Scrub **18.9 → 30.6s**.

## How it's timed

Every element lands on the word that names it — that's the part the three rings never did:

| VO word | | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| 22.81 | *decomposes* | `DECOMPOSE` appears |
| 24.31 | *runs a step* | `RUN A STEP` + the arrow down |
| 25.23 | *evaluates* | `EVALUATE` + the second arrow |
| 26.70 | *re-plans* | **the red return edge draws itself** |
| 27.20–27.86 | *repeats* | the highlight travels the cycle twice |
| 28.32 | *until* | the green exit opens |
| 29.02 | *met.* | `CONDITION MET` |

The return edge draws as **one continuous stroke** across all three of its segments rather than three bars switching on, so it reads as a line finding its way back. It's also the only red in the frame. And the highlight runs the cycle **twice** before the exit is allowed to open — a loop that runs once isn't a loop.

## Two decisions I made that you should check

**The panel is now a push-in.** No monitor. The screen becomes the frame, because boxes can't be this size inside a 370px screen rect. The cut goes dev-at-desk → full-bleed diagram → close on dev, which reads as pushing in and pulling back out. I rendered the frames either side to confirm it doesn't jar.

**I kept the log, as a band along the bottom.** Prop 2 as mocked had no log. I kept it because you said the terminal was deliberate — *"I purposely put the terminal to show that the developer did not understand it"* — and that's the thing protecting the pivot two panels later. Structure, not simplification: confident labelled boxes over a log nobody can follow.

If you'd rather have pure prop 2, it's one line — `SCREEN_RECT["h"] = 0` in `spec.py`.

## Where it lives

All of it is data. `spec.FLOW` holds the geometry and every timing; `spec.SCREEN_RECT` holds the log band; `emit.py` writes them into `cut.json`; the `Flow` component in `LoopV10.tsx` reads them. **No timing is in the TSX.** The old `ICONS` block and the `Icons` component are gone.

Frame 15's AI-mark byline moved to `(642, 898)` at 66px — its old corner is where the flowchart now runs.

Two things still outstanding: the **plain panel** (you haven't picked — I'd said rabbit-plus-the-vacuum's-failure-beat), and this change is **uncommitted** in `comics-video`. Say the word and I'll commit it.

## The panel now runs in two stages

**Stage 1 — 18.9 to 22.55s.** The monitor, with the mark centred on it, exactly like every other frame where the AI speaks. It holds through the jargon: *"iterative execution against a termination condition."*

**Stage 2 — 22.55 to 30.6s.** Monitor gone, screen becomes the frame, diagram builds, mark small in the bottom right.

**The cut lands on 22.55s, which is between "It" (22.63) and "decomposes" (22.81).** That turned out to be the best thing about your note — the cut now sits exactly where the AI stops reciting and starts explaining. It's motivated rather than arbitrary, and the first box arrives 0.26s after the push, so the new frame isn't empty for long.

It's a hard cut, no dissolve. Same rule as every other panel boundary.

## What changed underneath

The terminal is gone entirely — `SCREEN_LOG` deleted, the `Screen` component deleted, the rule and the band gone. **The old log is in git at `c83d3f4`** if the dense version is ever wanted back, and `spec.py` says so at the point where it used to live.

With the band gone the flowchart got the whole frame, so I respaced it: boxes at `370 / 584 / 798` instead of `330 / 500 / 670`, pill down at `990`, return edge from `856` up to `642`. The corner mark sits at `(648, 1170)` at 66px, below the pill in space that used to be log.

The push is data too — `spec.PUSH` carries the cut time, the background and where the mark goes afterwards. `Plate` gained a `pushFrom` so any panel can push in later, not just this one.

I misread my own contact sheet at thumbnail size and thought the return edge was firing early, so I counted red pixels in the edge corridor frame by frame: **0 at 26.67s, 1,386 at 26.87s, 3,066 at 27.07s.** It draws exactly on *"re-plans"* and nowhere earlier.

## ASSISTANT
Counter's in. Studio rebuilt on its own — refresh **http://localhost:3334** and scrub 22.8 → 30.6s.

## How it runs

| VO | Counter |
|---|---|
| 22.81 *decomposes* | `0/3` appears, three empty pips |
| 25.30 *evaluates the result* | `1/3` |
| 26.70 *re-plans* | **goes red, shakes once, stays at `1/3`** |
| 27.38 | `2/3` |
| 27.96 | `3/3` |
| 29.02 *met.* | the whole thing turns green |

Sits at the top right, beside `DECOMPOSE`, with a `STEPS DONE` label above it and **three pips below** — the number for the read, the pips for the glance.

## The one design decision in there

**On the failure it holds. It doesn't go up, and it doesn't go down.**

That hold is the actual teach: the check gates progress, so a failed step costs you a lap round the loop and buys you nothing. If the counter climbed on every attempt it would be measuring effort instead of results, which is the opposite of what "evaluates the result" means.

A number that stays the same is invisible, though — so it flashes red and takes **one shake** on that beat. That gives you an event without lying about the value. It fires on `26.70`, the same instant the red return edge starts drawing, so the fail and the re-plan read as one thing rather than two.

It also appears as `0/3` the moment `DECOMPOSE` lands, which is what tells you where the three came from.

All of it is in `spec.FLOW["count"]` — positions, the step times, the fail window, the done time. Nothing in the TSX.

Still open: the **plain panel** pick, and everything since the initial commit is uncommitted. That's now three changes deep on frame 15 — worth a commit whenever you want one.

Yes — and it needs fixing more than the visual does. Here's what's wrong with it.

## The current line

> *"You give it a finish line. It does one thing, checks whether it's there yet, does the next thing. Nobody in the middle."*

**It has no failure in it.** The technical panel now has a red return edge and a counter that holds at `1/3` when a check fails. The plain version has nothing going wrong at all — so the *simple* explanation is now less complete than the hard one, which is backwards. `comic-animation.md` is blunt about this: *"The failure beat is the one worth building… without a failure, 're-plans' has nothing to attach to."*

**The metaphor is travel, and the mechanism isn't.** "Finish line", "there yet" — a loop isn't a journey toward a place, it's the same check run again until it passes. That metaphor is exactly what produced the dot-and-flag, the weakest visual in the film. Fix the words and the visual problem partly solves itself.

**"Does one thing… does the next thing."** "Thing" twice, neither concrete — and it describes doing *different* tasks in sequence, when the actual mechanism is *the same check, repeatedly*.

**It never says you set the stopping condition.** That's the setup for *"You've had this the whole time"* four panels later, for the step-cap beat, and for the credits punchline. The condition is the thing that costs money when you get it wrong.

## Options

All ~25 words, matching the current 23 in 8.9s, with the payoff last so the disintegration still lands on it.

**A** — *"You tell it when to stop. It tries. It checks. Gets it wrong, tries again. It stops when the check passes. Nobody in the middle."*

**B** — *"You tell it when to stop. Then it just goes. Tries, checks, gets it wrong, goes again. It doesn't need you. Nobody in the middle."*

**C** — *"You tell it what done means. It tries, checks, tries again. It doesn't stop when you leave. It stops when it's right. Nobody in the middle."*

**I'd take A.** Four flat declaratives then the payoff, which is this AI's actual register — compare its best line in the series, *"It runs out of room. Oldest thing goes first. Go to sleep."* `Gets it wrong, tries again` is the failure in four words, and `It stops when the check passes` is the termination condition without ever naming it. No jargon, and "loop" never appears — the AI describes, it never names.

## What this costs, so you can decide when

**This isn't a text edit.** The line is a recorded ElevenLabs take, so it means a new generation on David (`rWV5HleMkWb5oluMwkA7`) — cheap, 30–50 credits — but new audio means new word times, and every animation beat in that panel is keyed to the old ones: three hops at `40.68 / 41.95 / 43.15`, arrival at `43.8`, and the human pushed out at `45.23`, which is currently *exactly* on the word "middle." All of it gets re-derived, and a 25-word line runs ~0.8s longer, so the tail after f20 shifts too.

Call it an hour: regenerate, measure the envelope, re-map the beats, re-time.

**So don't do it twice.** You still haven't picked the plain *visual*, and the metaphor in the line decides it — if it's the rabbit, the line should be about trying and checking, not about a finish line. Pick the line and the visual together, then I regenerate once and re-time once.

## How to show the agent does it: give the work a body that isn't a logo

Three boxes lighting up on their own is a *process* diagram. Nothing in it says something is doing the work, and nothing says it's doing it without you. That's the gap.

**Put a token through the chart.** One small neutral chip — a rounded rectangle, teal, no eye, no petals, nothing that could be called a logo. It enters at `DECOMPOSE`, and after that box lands **it splits into three** and they queue. Each one takes its turn: down into `RUN A STEP`, down into `EVALUATE`, then either out the bottom or **round the red edge and back up to try again**.

That one mechanic carries four of the five ideas in the VO line:

| VO | What the token does |
|---|---|
| *decomposes the task* | one chip becomes three |
| *runs a step* | a chip enters `RUN A STEP` |
| *evaluates the result* | it stops in `EVALUATE` and is held there |
| *re-plans, and repeats* | it travels the red edge back up and re-enters |

And it fixes the thing the panel is now missing: with the counter moving to the top, the chart has no moving part for the eye to follow. The token *is* that part, and it's countable — three chips, and you can see how many are left.

**Agency comes from movement with no hand in frame.** Add one cursor press at the very start of stage 2, then let the cursor leave. After that nothing enters the frame and the chips keep going on their own. `comic-animation.md`'s rule is exactly this — *absence needs presence*: you have to see somebody there for their not being there to register.

If you want a second signal for almost nothing: **a clock in the corner**, ticking through the run. It says "this happened while you weren't watching" better than any icon, and it plants the outro.

## One correction to your Slack reply

You wrote that the colour switching was matched to the VO where it says it re-evaluates and goes back and forth. That's accurate — I keyed it to the words. But **VO-accurate and legible aren't the same thing**, and Anandhi's complaint survives the defence: in the 1.9 seconds from 26.70 to 28.60 there are **16 discrete visual changes, one every 119 ms**, including **four box-highlight switches**.

Two boxes alternating colour four times reads as a strobe. **One object travelling the same round trip reads as a round trip** — same VO sync, same beats, completely different legibility. That's the argument to make back, rather than dropping the sync.

## The layout, with captions gone

Captions out frees the top third, so:

- **Counter to the top**, where the caption sat — big, `STEPS DONE 1/3` with the three pips. It's now the panel's title rather than a detail beside a box.
- **Boxes get the middle**, and can grow, since they no longer share the frame with a caption.
- **`CONDITION MET` stays at the bottom**, with room above it now.
- **No mark anywhere.** Stage 1 is just the monitor — which is enough, because he asked that monitor a question twenty seconds earlier and it answered him. The AI's identity is already established by context; it never needed a badge.

## Slower, with fewer events

Four events in stage 2 instead of sixteen: **boxes draw and hold** · **chips split** · **one chip fails and travels back** · **exit opens**. The boxes become the static — once drawn they never change again. Only the chips move and only the counter climbs.

## The gap in the plan

**Removing captions from the explainer doesn't answer Anandhi's point 4.** Hers was about the whole video — *"I don't know if any of the captions are landing"* — and across the full 197 words the median is **299 ms, with 30% under 200 ms**. Dropping them from two panels fixes the explainer and leaves the other thirty. If you're getting approvals tomorrow, that one should be listed as still open rather than closed, or it'll come back.

Short answer: **technical line keeps its words, plain line doesn't.**

## Don't touch the technical VO's words

Three reasons, and they all point the same way.

**It's supposed to be impenetrable.** It's the line that earns *"say that again like I'm not an engineer"* two panels later. Make it clearer and the pivot has nothing to pivot from. It's also the one place the skill allows jargon — the dense rung.

**With captions gone, the VO and the labels now reinforce each other.** The line says *decomposes… runs a step… evaluates* and the boxes say `DECOMPOSE` / `RUN A STEP` / `EVALUATE`. Hearing the word as the word appears is exactly what makes the panel readable with sound on *and* off. Changing the VO would break that pairing.

**And it answers Anandhi's point 1 for free.** She asked whether the captions could just be part of the screen. In the flowchart they already are — `DECOMPOSE`, `RUN A STEP`, `EVALUATE`, `STEPS DONE 2/3`, `CONDITION MET`. That's the caption, welded into the diagram. Worth saying back to her in those words, because it's her own suggestion already built.

## But it does need re-spacing — and that's free

The pace problem isn't the words, it's where they sit. The back half is bunched: **from *re-plans* at 26.70 to *met.* at 29.02, four ideas in 2.32 seconds**, with gaps of 0.46 / 0.40 / 0.30 / 0.46 / 0.18s. The front half has a 1.32s pause after *condition.* and room to spare. The VO itself front-loads and then sprints.

So insert silence **inside the take** — after *result,*, after *re-plans,*, and after *repeats* — about 0.9s total. Same performance, same words, no regeneration. `AI.mp3` gets sliced to its own file the way `intro_long.mp3` and `sigh.mp3` already were, which the audio skill wants anyway: don't seek into an mp3 for a clip you care about.

**And it costs nothing downstream.** Frame 16, straight after the panel, is **1.30 seconds of silent beat frame**. Extend f15 to ~31.5s and shorten f16 to ~0.4s and the 0.9s is absorbed. **Nothing after it moves.** No re-time of the film, no re-measure, no new credits.

That plus the token and fewer events is the explainer closed today, as you told her.

## The plain line does need changing

Everything I said before still stands — no failure in it, the finish-line metaphor is a journey when the mechanism is a retry, "thing" twice — and taking the captions off makes it more urgent, not less, because the line becomes the only verbal channel for the plain explanation.

But it's **not a today job**, and here's the constraint: f20's audio runs 38.40–45.48 with the scream layered at 45.45, and f21 starts at 47.30. There's **0.15s of slack**. A longer line shifts everything after it, so that one is a real regenerate-and-re-time pass — and it should be decided together with the visual, since the line picks the metaphor and the metaphor picks the picture.

## One thing captions-off creates that you should decide on

The technical panel survives muted because the diagram is made of words. **The plain panel currently has no on-screen text at all** — just the dot and the flag. If captions come off that one too, a muted viewer gets *nothing* from the simple explanation, on a channel where most feed viewing is muted.

So whichever plain visual you pick has to carry its own labels. The rabbit strip already does — *hop* / *lifts the lid — empty* / *carrot. it stops.* Worth checking against that requirement rather than picking on charm.

## What the panel does now

**The boxes are statics.** They draw once on the word that names them and then never change again — no highlight, no colour switching. That kills Anandhi's point 3 at the root: nothing blinks.

**A chip does the moving, and its position is the highlight.** `DECOMPOSE` produces three chips. One at a time they ride down the right-hand lane into `RUN A STEP`, get held in `EVALUATE`, and are either absorbed — counter ticks — or sent back round. The chip sits on each box's right *border*, never over its label.

**The failing chip drives the road.** On *re-plans* the red edge draws itself and the chip immediately travels it: down, out, up, back into `RUN A STEP`. It's red for exactly that round trip. **The edge is the road, the chip is the car** — they share the route on purpose.

That's also the answer to your question about showing the agent. A flowchart alone is a process diagram; it could be a build script. **Three chips that split off and ride the lane with no hand in frame** is what says something is doing the work and nobody's watching it.

**Counter's at the top**, where the caption was — `STEPS DONE` with the fraction at 84px and three pips. It's the panel's title now.

**No caption, no logo.** The panel survives muted because the diagram is made of words: `DECOMPOSE`, `RUN A STEP`, `EVALUATE`, `STEPS DONE 2/3`, `CONDITION MET`. And the AI's identity was established twenty seconds earlier when Dev 1 asked this monitor a question and it answered — it never needed a badge.

## The slow-down cost nothing

Measured the take rather than trusting the transcript: the only real pauses in that line are at `3.17`, `5.00`, `5.92`, `7.42`. **There is no silence at all after "re-plans"** — *"re-plans, and repeats until the condition is met"* is delivered as one unbroken phrase, so it can't be padded internally. That's why chasing each word in it was always going to strobe.

So I widened the two real pauses instead: **+0.25s after "task," and +0.30s after "result,"**, giving 450ms and 460ms of air where there was 200 and 160. Same performance, no regeneration — `ai_tech.mp3` is `AI.mp3` sliced with silence spliced in.

**And the 0.55s came out of frame 16**, the silent beat frame right after, which drops from 1.30s to 0.75s. `END` is still `104.33` and the film is still `3130` frames. **Nothing downstream moved.**

## Two things to know for tomorrow

**The mark is off frame 15 only.** The other eight AI frames still carry it. Removing those is one line, but it's a bigger call than the explainer and I didn't want to make it for you an hour before the meeting.

**Frame 20's caption is still there, deliberately.** The plain panel has no on-screen words at all — just the dot and the flag — so stripping its caption before the visual is redesigned would leave muted viewers nothing. Worth saying in the room that the explainer is closed and the plain panel is the next piece, rather than presenting both as done.

## Stage 1: full sentence, logo in the monitor

*"Iterative execution against a termination condition."* arrives **as one complete sentence** above the monitor, holds for 3.4 seconds, then goes on the cut. Not word by word. The mark is back, centred on the monitor, exactly as it was — and it goes when the monitor goes, so it never appears over the diagram.

New `HOLD_CAPTIONS` block in `spec.py` — it's a general mechanism, so any panel can take a held sentence now, not just this one.

## Stage 2: no captions, and genuinely slower

**Two steps, not three.** This is the real fix for "too fast" and it's worth knowing why. I measured what each chip was actually getting:

| | on screen |
|---|---|
| chip 1 | 1.79s |
| chip 2 | 2.28s |
| chip 3 | **0.60s** |

The third chip was three times faster than the first. That unevenness is what you were feeling — and it wasn't fixable by stretching, because the VO's back half (*"re-plans, and repeats until the condition is met"*) is **one unbroken 2.32-second phrase with no silence in it to pad**. Three journeys plus a round trip cannot fit in that at an even tempo.

Two chips, identical shape each — queue → run (0.50s) → hold → evaluate (0.55s) → hold → absorbed — with the failure landing on *re-plans*. Nothing is rushed, and the whole mechanism is still demonstrated: a step runs, gets checked, fails, goes round, runs again, then the last one goes straight through. **Decomposing into two parts is still decomposing.** It's `total=2` in the spec if you want three back.

**The panel went from 12.25s to 14.70s.** I couldn't pay for all of that out of the beat frame this time, so everything from frame 17 shifted +2.10s. Film is now **106.43s**, and I checked the loop seam survives — `dev2_exhausted` still runs to exactly `END`.

The AI stops talking at 30.03s but the diagram keeps working until 33.60 — **3.57 seconds of the machine finishing in silence**, with only ambience under it.

I left it deliberately, because it's the panel's own argument: the voice stops, nobody is there, and the thing carries on and completes by itself. That's *"nobody in the middle"* demonstrated two panels before it's stated.

But it is a long hold for a Shorts-paced cut, and it's the first thing I'd trim if it drags on a phone. Tightening it means speeding the failed chip's round trip, which costs about a second.

## ASSISTANT
It makes sense, and on the agent question it's the best answer we've had. On the loop question — no, and it's worth fixing before I build it.

## What it does well

**Agency is unambiguous.** The traveller *is* the AI, so there's no gap between "something is doing this" and "the AI is doing this". The chip never managed that — a neutral rounded rectangle needed the boxes around it to mean anything. One logo popping into three and the three doing the work says decomposition and agency in a single move.

**Three lanes suits the frame.** 9:16 gives you three stacked rows with ~460px of travel each, plus room for the counter. That's a better use of the format than anything we've tried.

## Where it doesn't work: the loop

**Movement is left → right, one way.** A loop is a *return*. "Fails midway and restarts" reads as **start over**, which is close but not the same thing — and with three lanes completing in sequence, the dominant read is a **download manager**: three parallel tasks finishing. The loop becomes a detail inside lane 2 rather than the subject.

**The cheap fix: make the failed attempt come back on a visibly different path.** Out along the top of its lane, and when it fails, back along the bottom — in red. The lane then draws a **closed circuit**, and you've seen a loop rather than a rewind. Same choreography, one extra arc.

**And let the counter carry the meaning:** lane 2 makes *two* journeys for *one* tick. Two attempts, one increment. That's what "evaluates the result" actually means — the check gates progress — and it's the thing the current version teaches best.

## Three things to change in the plan as you wrote it

**The ticks and the counter are the same information twice.** Three ticks appearing on the right *is* a 3/3 counter. That doubling is what made the last version busy. Keep both only if the ticks are quiet — small, and clearly per-lane — with the counter as the aggregate. Otherwise pick one.

**There's no destination.** They "move to the right side and get a tick", so arrival is implied by stopping. Give them something to arrive *at* — a vertical line, a marker — and "until the condition is met" becomes visible instead of inferred. Right now nothing on screen represents the condition.

**The pop-out needs a beat.** One logo → three is the decomposition, and it lands on *"decomposes the task"*. If it happens fast it reads as a glitch. Hold the single logo for ~0.3s first, then split.

## The thing that actually decides this

**Anandhi rejected the logo.** *"I don't think what we intend to do here with a Claude like logo works."* This choreography makes the mark the entire panel — every moving element is a copy of it. If her point stands, the whole thing is built on a rejected element, and you'd be showing it to her tomorrow.

That's not a craft problem I can fix in the file. It's the open decision from her feedback, and it's the one I'd settle before building this. Two ways through it: get her to sign off on the mark specifically because it's now load-bearing, or change the mark's shape so it stops reading as Claude's — six petals plus a centre dot is genuinely close, and a different silhouette would keep the whole choreography intact.

## How I'd do it

**Let me mock it in Figma first, not Remotion.** Three states in one frame — split, lane 2 failing on its return arc, all three ticked at 3/3 — the same way you picked prop 2 this morning. Five minutes instead of half an hour, and you've had three Remotion passes now that felt odd on arrival. If the mock reads right, I'll build it properly.

Want me to mock it with the current mark, or with a couple of alternative silhouettes alongside so you can take the logo question to her with options?

omg, how can we even do this. I am sick of it, please come up with a new way to represent which seems simple yet complex that can be visually represented easily, can visualize a loop that an agent carries out.

## ASSISTANT
Fair. Here's one answer, and I think it's the obvious one we've been walking past.

## Draw the loop as a loop

**A ring. One dot going round it. A counter in the middle.**

Every version so far drew a loop as something that isn't loop-shaped — a flowchart with a return edge, then three lanes going left to right. Both needed the viewer to *infer* the repetition. A dot travelling a circle doesn't need inferring.

**Two marks on the ring:** `RUN` at the top, `CHECK` on the right. **The counter sits inside the ring**, which is where it belongs — it's the loop's state. **An exit at the bottom** goes down to a `DONE` pill, and it stays shut until the counter fills.

## What the viewer sees

| VO | On screen |
|---|---|
| *decomposes the task* | the ring draws, with three ticks on it — three things to do |
| *runs a step* | the dot passes `RUN` |
| *evaluates the result* | the dot reaches `CHECK`, green, counter → `1/3` |
| *re-plans* | next lap, `CHECK` goes **red** — and the counter doesn't move |
| *and repeats* | it goes round again anyway |
| *until the condition is met* | `3/3`, the exit opens, the dot leaves the ring |

**Four laps, three ticks.** That gap is the whole teach — you watch it do a lap for nothing. "Evaluates the result" stops being a phrase and becomes the reason a lap was wasted.

## Why this fixes what's been breaking

**One object, one path, eight seconds.** Nothing else moves. No colour switching, no boxes appearing, no lanes, no strobe. The problem in every version has been simultaneous change, and a dot on a ring has none.

**The agent is the dot, and the dot is nobody's.** No hand, no cursor, no logo. **It doesn't depend on the mark at all** — which takes you out of the fight with Anandhi entirely. If you later want the dot to *be* the mark, it drops in; if not, it stays a dot and nothing breaks.

**It reads technical, not cute.** A ring with checkpoints looks like a state machine, which is the right register for the hard explanation — and it stays clearly opposite to whatever the plain version becomes.

**It's the cheapest thing yet to draw.** A stroked circle, a dot positioned by angle, three ticks, two labels, a counter, an exit. Fewer elements than what's in there now.

And it rhymes with the episode: the stamp already says `LOOP · CLEARED`, and now the screen shows a loop clearing.

---

Give me five minutes and I'll mock it in Figma — three states, so you can see the lap, the red check, and the exit opening — before I touch Remotion again.

In the file as **`RING 1`**, **`RING 2`**, **`RING 3`**, below the plain-explanation row.

## What's on screen

**One ring.** `RUN A STEP` is a gate at the top, `CHECK` is a gate at the right. The count lives **inside** the loop, because it's the loop's state. The way out hangs off the bottom and is **shut** — a grey outline — until the count fills.

**One dot goes round.** That's the only thing that ever moves.

## The three states

**1 — a lap passes.** The dot reaches `CHECK`, the gate is green, the count goes to `1/3`.

**2 — the check fails.** The gate goes red, the dot's whole path lights up ahead of it, and **the count stays at `1/3`**. It's about to do the entire lap again and get nothing for it. That's the beat that teaches — you watch a lap get spent.

**3 — the count is full.** `3/3`, the gate at the bottom opens, the dot leaves the ring.

## Why I think this one holds

**It's a loop because it's a circle.** Every version before this asked the viewer to infer repetition from a return edge or a restarting lane. Nothing to infer here.

**One object, one path, no strobe.** That's what broke the last three: too many things changing at once. There's nothing else to change.

**No logo.** The dot is a dot. That takes this panel completely out of the argument with Anandhi — and if you later want the dot to *be* the mark, it drops straight in and nothing else moves.

**Four laps, three ticks.** The count advancing three times over four laps is the entire meaning of "evaluates the result", made arithmetic.

## Two things I fixed in the mock, worth knowing

`CHECK` was outside the ring at x 631–709, which is **inside the Shorts right rail** — the last 110px that carry like/comment/share. It's inside the ring now, right-aligned against the gate. And the dot was overlapping the `DONE` pill by 8px in state 3.

## Small calls left

`DONE` versus `CONDITION MET` — the VO says *"until the condition is met"*, so the longer label ties to the words, but `DONE` is calmer on a gate. And the count: three is right if the VO's "decomposes" should mean three parts, but four laps for three ticks needs the ring's timing checked against the 8 seconds we have. That's a spec detail, not a design one.

If this reads right to you, say so and I'll build it — and this time the whole panel is a circle, a dot, two gates and a number, so it's less code than what's in there now.

## Decomposition — you were right, it wasn't there

The ring drawing isn't decomposition, and two pips fading in isn't one thing *becoming* two. Now: the ring arrives, **one dot appears at its centre — the task — and divides**, the halves travel out and settle as the two step markers, and **the count appears because there is now something to count.**

That sequencing also fixed a collision I'd have shipped: the split was running straight through the middle of `0/2`. Holding the count back until the split lands means nothing shares that space.

## The ending doesn't turn a corner any more

The dot was travelling the ring and then instantly heading straight down — a 90° direction change with nothing easing it. That's what you were seeing.

**Now the dot never leaves.** It stops on its last check, with one small settle so a dead stop reads as *arrived*. Then the ending is carried by **colour spreading instead of an object moving**: the ring fills green all the way round, the green runs down the exit, the gate lights. A colour has no direction to change.

It's also more truthful — *"until the condition is met"* looks like stopping, not like going somewhere else.

## The blank face was my regression

It was 1.30s originally. I cut it to 0.40s to pay for the panel extension and never gave it back when I trimmed the panel down again. **It's 1.50s now** — more than it started with, since it's the series' signature image and the joke needs the hold. I've left a note in `timeline.py` not to trim it again. Film is 105.53s.

---

## On changing the VO

The animation now does: **splits · runs · checks · fails · goes round · stops.** The current line says *"decomposes the task, runs a step, evaluates the result, re-plans, and repeats until the condition is met"* — which is close, but *"repeats"* is abstract where the screen is literally going round, and the failure only gets the word *"re-plans"*.

What I'd change, keeping the jargon opening intact:

> **"Iterative execution against a termination condition. It decomposes the task, runs a step, evaluates the result. It fails, it goes round, it runs it again. It stops when every step has passed."**

Three short declaratives in the middle, which is this AI's actual register — compare *"It runs out of room. Oldest thing goes first. Go to sleep."* And every beat on screen now has a word: `decomposes` → the split, `runs a step` → the gate, `evaluates the result` → the check, **`it fails` → the red gate**, **`it goes round` → the lap**, `it stops` → the halt, `every step has passed` → the count filling.

**I'd keep `decomposes`, `evaluates` and `termination condition`.** They're the dense rung — the whole point is that he can't follow it. Swapping them for "splits" and "checks" would make the technical explanation plain and leave the pivot two panels later with nothing to pivot from.

**The cost is real, though.** The ring's lap of 2.32s isn't a guess — it's *solved* from "re-plans" at 27.25 and "met." at 29.57. New audio means new anchors, so I'd regenerate, re-measure the envelope and re-solve the lap and all three checks. Maybe 40 minutes, and it can't be padded out of the beat frame this time.

Worth doing, but not before tomorrow's meeting unless you want it. The current line already lands on the animation; the rewrite makes the failure a spoken beat rather than a single word.

## ASSISTANT
Yes — six "it"s in thirty-three words. That's my fault and it came from a specific mistake.

The original line uses **one subject governing a list of verbs**: *"It decomposes the task, runs a step, evaluates the result, re-plans, and repeats…"* — one "It" doing five things. When I broke it into short sentences to get the flat register, every sentence needed its own subject back, and that's where they all came from.

## Fix: drop the subjects entirely

> **"Iterative execution against a termination condition. Decomposes the task. Runs a step. Evaluates the result. Fails, goes round, runs again. Stops when every step has passed."**

**Zero "it".** And bare third-person verbs with no subject is exactly the right register here — that's how a man page reads, or a spec sheet. This AI reciting its own documentation at someone who asked a simple question is the joke, and *"Fails, goes round, runs again."* is as flat as it gets.

26 words against the current 27, so the timing anchors stay comparable and I wouldn't have to re-solve the lap by much.

## If you'd rather keep it as one sentence

> **"Iterative execution against a termination condition. It decomposes the task, runs a step, evaluates the result, fails, goes round, runs again, and stops when every step has passed."**

One "It", nothing repeated. Keeps the original's shape and just adds the failure to the list. Smoother, less brutal — safer if the fragments come back from ElevenLabs sounding like a shopping list.

## The one trade

The bare-verb version has **"step" twice** — *a step* and *every step*. I'd leave that: it's a concrete noun doing work, not a pronoun filling space, and it ties the words to the `STEPS DONE` label on screen.

**One thing to watch when you generate it:** short verb fragments tend to come back with rising list intonation, which would fight the deadpan. Worth a tag pushing it flat and slightly bored, and worth generating the middle three fragments as one line rather than separately so the pacing between them is the performance rather than my splice.

how does this relate with the agent working on it?

## ASSISTANT
Good catch — that's a bigger problem than the "it"s, and my fix caused it.

**Dropping the subject drops the agent.** *"Decomposes the task. Runs a step."* has nobody doing it. A listener supplies "the loop does X", which makes it a **process** — and process is exactly what this panel is fighting. Man pages read like that because they describe mechanisms, not actors. So I traded a repetition problem for an agency problem, on the one panel whose job is agency.

## The fix is first person

> **"Iterative execution against a termination condition. I decompose the task. Run a step. Evaluate the result. Fail, go round, run it again. Stop when every step has passed."**

One **"I"** at the top sets the subject and every verb after it inherits it. **One "I", one "it", 28 words** — the repetition is gone, the flat documentation rhythm survives, and the actor is named once and unmistakably.

And it's a stronger claim than the visual can make alone. The dot shows *something* acts by itself; **"I"** says *the thing he is talking to* acts by itself. That second claim is the episode — he's four panels away from realising he could have been doing this all along, and *"You've had this the whole time"* lands harder if the machine has spent the last ten seconds describing its own work.

## It's already this AI's voice

`cast.md` has it saying *"You clicked yes. **I'm** locked in. Go to sleep."* First person is established for Dev 1's AI, so this isn't a new register — and a burnt-out machine flatly listing its own chores, including *"Fail"*, is exactly the character. **"I fail"** as a deadpan admission is funnier than "it fails", and it lands on the red gate.

The series rule that the AIs aren't characters is about faces and avatars, not pronouns. Saying "I" doesn't make it a character; it makes it the thing that's talking.

## Where the words meet the ring

| Word | On screen |
|---|---|
| *I decompose the task* | the dot divides in two |
| *Run a step* | it passes `RUN A STEP` |
| *Evaluate the result* | it reaches `CHECK` |
| **Fail** | **the gate goes red, the count holds** |
| *go round* | the lap it spends for nothing |
| *run it again* | the next pass |
| *Stop when every step has passed* | it halts, the ring greens, the gate opens |

Every beat on the ring has a word, and the words now have an owner.

One **"I"** at the top; every verb after it inherits the subject, including the conditional. **One "I", two "it", 31 words** — your conditional survives, the telegraphic rhythm holds all the way through, and the actor is still named.

The VO says **"Evaluate the result"** while the gate on screen says **`CHECK`**. Two panels later he asks for it in plain English and it's been on the screen the whole time.

Same reason I'd keep *decompose*, *iterative execution* and *termination condition*: that's the dense rung, and *"say that again like I'm not an engineer"* needs something to be said again.

Six phrases, one per sentence, with **480–700ms between them** — the fragments landed as fragments, not as a rising list. Level is −18.0dB mean against `AI.mp3`'s −18.6, so no normalising either. ## The lap was solved, not chosen

Two words are load-bearing: **"fail" at 28.55** and **"passed" at 32.00**. Those fix the lap at **3.45s — 104°/s**, which is travelling speed rather than a spin. And that number then put the dot's entrance at **25.39**, inside *"Run a step"*, without me placing it.

## One geometry change the take forced

**`CHECK` moved from 3 o'clock to 10 o'clock.** At 3 o'clock the first check arrived a quarter-lap in, which left no room for the VO's pacing between *"Run a step"* and *"If I fail"*. At 10 o'clock the **long arc of the circle sits between the two gates**, so the travel itself is the work and the check is what you hit at the end of it — and you finish where you started, which reinforces the loop rather than fighting it.

That did collide `CHECK`'s label with `RUN A STEP`'s. It's outside the ring on the upper left now, clear of it, and the left edge carries no Shorts UI.

## One decision you should know about

**The count isn't tied one-to-one to the checks any more.** The take shows one failure and one pass — but the line ends *"when every step has passed"*, so both markers fill on that phrase, the second a beat after the first. The checks are the mechanism; the count is the outcome.

If you'd rather the count only moved on a visible check, it'd have to be `0/1 → 1/1`, which is a silly-looking counter. This was the better of the two.

**Film is 108.13s** — the take is 2.6s longer than the one it replaced, which is most of the growth. I'll flag when the render's down.

## The dot now waits to be judged

That was the actual mistake: it rotated at one constant speed and got its verdict wherever it happened to be. **"Evaluate the result" is when it *arrives*** — the verdict comes afterwards, while it sits at the gate.

So the motion is keyframed now — travel, hold, travel, hold:

| | |
|---|---|
| **25.29** | leaves `RUN` — *"Run a step"* |
| **27.12** | **arrives** `CHECK`, 330° later — *"Evaluate the result"* |
| 27.12 → 29.10 | waits at the gate |
| **28.55** | verdict: **FAIL** — on the word *"fail"* |
| **29.10 → 31.10** | **a full revolution** — *"go round and run it again"* |
| 31.10 → 32.00 | waits again |
| **32.00** | verdict: **PASS** — on *"passed"* |

**Two revolutions, one per attempt**, both at the same 180°/s. The holds are what punctuate it — the dot arrives, is examined, and gets an answer, which is what a check actually is.

That also fixed the pace complaint without changing the speed: the problem wasn't how fast it moved, it was that it never stopped, so nothing on screen corresponded to being evaluated.

The verdicts land on their words rather than near them — `28.55` sits inside *"If I fail"* (28.18–28.85) and `32.00` inside *"Stop when every step has passed"* (30.73–32.61), with `passed` as the last word.

Film's unchanged at **108.13s** — this was motion only, no retiming. I'll flag when the render's down.

Nothing was removed — **I broke it, and you caught it.** Good question to ask.

## What happened

`spec.PLAIN` held **absolute film times**: hops at `40.68 / 41.95 / 43.15`, arrival at `43.80`. Frame 20 used to start at 38.40, so those were 2.3–5.4 seconds into its own panel.

Four retimes of the technical panel later, **frame 20 starts at 44.10** — and I shifted the timeline entries every time but never those numbers. So every hop, ping and the arrival were firing **5.7 seconds before the panel opened.** The panel came up with the mark already parked at the flag, held for a second, and then the human got X'd out. The hopping never played at all.

## And it wasn't only that

**The `click` lettering was broken the same way.** Its hits sat at `52.95–57.85`, absolute, for the typing shot — which now starts at **58.95**. Six seconds early, so the word "click" was appearing over the plain explanation instead of over the keyboard.

Two regressions from one cause, and neither would ever have shown up in the technical panel I was staring at.

## The fix isn't +5.70

Adding the offset would have worked until the next retime. **Every timing in `PLAIN`, `SFX` and `HOLD_CAPTIONS` is now an offset from its own panel's start**, and `emit.py` resolves them against the timeline once. A retime of any earlier panel can't desync them again — that's what `RING` already did, which is why the technical panel survived four of them untouched.

I ran an audit across the whole cut afterwards: **every timing now sits inside its own frame.** That check is worth keeping as a habit — it would have caught this four passes ago.

Rendering now. Worth watching the plain panel end to end when it lands, since it hasn't actually played correctly in several versions and I'd rather you saw it than took my word for it.

> **"You tell me what done looks like. Then I go, check, go, check. Get it wrong, do it again. Nobody in the middle."**

**24 words. One "I", one "you".** Same elision trick as the technical fix — one subject, then the verbs inherit it.

## Why "You tell me" matters more than the pronoun tidy-up

The current line is *"You give **it** a finish line."* That's the machine describing a transaction between him and some third thing. **"You tell me"** makes it an exchange between the two people actually in the scene.

And that's the setup for **"WAIT. YOU CAN DO THAT??"** two frames later. His realisation isn't *"loops exist"* — it's *"I could have been the one telling it."* The line has to put him in the sentence for that to land.

## On the vagueness

*"It does one thing… does the next thing"* is vague because **"thing" isn't anything** — and it says the same word twice. `go, check, go, check` is the same length and it's what's on screen: hop, ping, hop, ping. Four syllables doing what nine were failing to do.

And *"Get it wrong, do it again"* adds the failure, which the plain version currently doesn't have at all. That was already backwards — the simple explanation being *less* complete than the hard one.

**"Nobody in the middle"** stays exactly as it is. It's the best line in the film, it's what the emoji disintegration lands on, and the whole visual beat is built on the word *"middle"*.

## ASSISTANT
Ignoring the current one entirely. Here's the answer I'd back.

## Bricks laid to a string line

**You stretch a taut string across the frame and walk out. A wall builds itself up to it, one brick at a time, checking each one against the string. One brick sits proud — it gets lifted out and re-laid. When the last brick meets the string, it stops.**

## Why this one and not the others

**The condition you set is visible in every single frame.** That's the thing every version of the technical panel fought and never won — "termination condition" was always a label, a pill, a word. Here it's a string line stretched across the shot for the whole panel. *"You tell me what done looks like"* isn't narrated, it's **drawn**, and it stays on screen while the work happens.

**The wall is the progress.** No counter, no fraction, no pips. Its height tells you how far along it is. That matters because the technical panel is now a ring with a `2/2` in it — the plain one having no number at all is exactly the contrast the two explanations need.

**"Get it wrong, do it again" is a physical event.** A brick proud of the line, lifted out, re-laid flush. Nobody has to be told what went wrong. Compare the current version, where the failure isn't in the panel at all.

**"Nobody in the middle" gets a real absence.** Someone ties the string and leaves — so the emoji disintegration lands on a person you actually watched be there, which is the animation skill's own rule and the thing the floating head never managed.

**And it's the cheapest thing on the list.** Bricks are rectangles. A string is a line. The only generated art is the person at the top, and the cast sheet already covers that.

## Beat map

| Line | On screen |
|---|---|
| *You tell me what done looks like* | a hand pulls the string taut, and leaves frame |
| *Then I go, check, go, check* | brick down · a check against the string · brick down · check |
| *Get it wrong, do it again* | one brick proud of the line — lifted out, re-laid |
| *Nobody in the middle* | wide on the finished wall, and the empty space where they stood |

## Two things it changes

**Warm colour, for the first time in the film.** Brick tones against a greyscale comic and a cold teal ring. That alone signals "this is the other explanation" before a word is spoken.

**The loop isn't this panel's job any more.** The ring already carried it. This one carries the *relationship* — you say what done is, then you're not needed — which is what the line actually says and what sets up *"WAIT. YOU CAN DO THAT??"* two frames later.

## One thing to decide

**Whether it needs any words.** The technical panel survives muted because it's built from labels. This one would be almost wordless — which is a strong contrast, but muted viewers get nothing from the teach. One label on the string line fixes it: `DONE`, or `THIS HIGH`.

I'd put `THIS HIGH` on it. It's plainer, it's funnier, and it sounds like something a person would actually say to a builder — which is the whole register of this half.

not sure it would be the best way to represent a loop

## ASSISTANT
You're right, and I was wrong about something upstream of the visual.

## What I got wrong

I said the loop wasn't this panel's job because the ring already carried it. That's backwards. **The technical panel is the one he doesn't understand — that's its entire purpose.** If the loop only exists in the panel designed to go over your head, then the episode's own word never gets explained in a form anyone retains. The plain panel is where the loop has to land, *more* than the technical one.

So the bricks were wrong for exactly the reason you say: a wall rising to a line is a **progress bar wearing a costume**. Monotonic, one-directional, and the re-laid brick is a local correction, not a cycle.

## The constraint that actually applies

Here's the bind, stated plainly: **the clearest way to show a loop is something going round — and the ring already took it.** If the plain panel also has a thing travelling a circular path, the two explanations look like the same panel twice, and the contrast between them is the whole pivot.

So the plain version has to show the loop as **repetition of an action**, not travel round a path. Loop in time, not loop in space.

## What I'd back instead: keys and a lock

**A ring of keys and a locked door. Key in, turn, no. Next key, turn, no. Next. Until one turns and the door opens.**

**The keyring is a literal loop it works its way round** — so you get the loop shape without a diagram, because a keyring is an object, not a chart. And every attempt is a discrete, repeated, self-checking action: does it turn or not? Nobody has to be told what the check is.

| Line | On screen |
|---|---|
| *You tell me what done looks like* | someone hangs the keyring by the locked door and walks off |
| *Then I go, check, go, check* | key in, turn, no · next key, turn, no |
| *Get it wrong, do it again* | the ring rotates, bringing the next key round |
| *Nobody in the middle* | the door swings open — and nobody is standing there |

**No counter needed.** How much of the keyring is left to try *is* the progress, the same way the wall was — except a keyring is genuinely a loop, so it doesn't smuggle in a progress bar.

**And it pays off the ending.** *"What happened to my AI credits?"* — it tried four hundred keys. A machine patiently working round a keyring all night is the same joke as *"left a loop running"*, and it makes the credits counter land as a consequence rather than a gag.

**Four elements:** door-and-lock, keyring, the key being tried, the person who leaves. Within the plain panel's budget, and only the person needs generated art.

## The honest weakness

It's **iteration over a list**, not strictly a while-loop — it terminates by finding the right key rather than by a condition being met. That's slightly different from what the AI is describing. If that bothers you, the alternative in this same family is **knocking on a door until someone answers**, which is a textbook loop and even plainer — but it has no visible loop object at all, so the repetition has to carry it entirely.

Say which and I'll mock it in Figma before anything goes near Remotion.

## USER
Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is part of this read — decided, not defaulted: a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, so it fits only when the user asked for Markdown or the content is bound for a Markdown-native destination; never pick it to save time.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the one font host the Artifact CSP admits — link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=…&display=swap">`); a face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI or it falls back silently. Either way, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* — most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token — a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Before publishing, scan the stylesheet for any color declared only inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — that is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject — or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name — a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word — a greeting, a category, a page-type label — the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## Handoff text — copy from here

> **Project:** *I Have No Idea What That Means* — a comic-strip explainer series for a company YouTube channel. One engineering term per episode, explained inside a joke. 9:16 vertical, 1–2 minutes, greyscale line art, burnt-in captions, no product content at all. Episode one is `loop`.
>
> **The premise:** someone uses a word, you nod because asking costs status, so you ask the AI instead, privately. Every episode runs the same engine — everyone is nodding, nobody is asking.
>
> **Structure of episode one:** Dev 2 says "left a loop running". Dev 1 nods, doesn't know what it means, asks his AI in private. The AI answers twice — once in jargon, then in plain words after he asks for it again. He tries it, it works, he goes home. The AI tries to warn him about the spend cap and he's already gone. Next morning he's the wrecked one saying "left a loop running", and Dev 3 nods at him. Ends on a credits counter at zero and the question "what happened to my AI credits?" — and the answer runs straight back into the opening line, so it loops.
>
> **Where it is:** shot, animated and cut. 46 frames, 110 seconds, built in Remotion where every timing lives in a spec file rather than a timeline, so the cut is rebuildable from the script.
>
> **The problem I've been solving all week.** The AI's technical explanation is delivered over a screen, and that screen has to visually explain what an agent loop is. Six attempts:
> 1. Three unlabelled icons in a row above the monitor. Review: *"just 3 icons, I didn't get the story at all."*
> 2. A labelled vertical stack. Better, but tiny — 44px glyphs squeezed above a monitor.
> 3. A flowchart with boxes and a red return edge. Review: abrupt, too much moving at once. Measured: 16 discrete visual changes in 1.9 seconds.
> 4. Chips travelling down lanes between the boxes. Same problem, and the third chip got 0.6s against the first chip's 1.8s.
> 5. Three copies of a logo racing left to right with ticks. Shows the agent well, shows the loop badly — reads as a download manager.
> 6. **What I've landed on:** draw the loop as a loop. One ring. "RUN A STEP" is a gate at the top, "CHECK" is a gate at ten o'clock, the step count sits inside the ring because it's the loop's state, and the exit hangs off the bottom shut until the count fills. One dot goes round. Three revolutions, three verdicts, one of them a failure — and on the failure the count doesn't move, so you watch a lap get spent for nothing.
>
> **Two rules that came out of it.** Let the recording set the beats — the lap length is solved from where the words "fail" and "passed" actually fall in the voice take, not chosen. And never advance a counter without a revolution behind it; an increment nothing caused reads as a glitch.
>
> **The VO for that panel:** *"Iterative execution against a termination condition. I decompose the task. Run a step. Evaluate the result. If I fail, go round and run it again. Stop when every step has passed."* Deliberately jargon-heavy — it's the explanation he doesn't understand, and it has to be impenetrable for "say that again like I'm not an engineer" to land two panels later. First person so the machine owns the work.
>
> **Still open:**
> - **The plain explanation.** Currently a dot travelling to a chequered flag, occupying about 180px of a 1280px frame. Being replaced. Line agreed but not recorded: *"You tell me what done looks like. Then I go, check, go, check. Get it wrong, do it again. Nobody in the middle."* The visual is undecided — candidates were a robot vacuum, a kettle, a rabbit checking a basket, bricks laid to a string line, and keys tried against a lock. The constraint: the technical panel already uses a thing travelling a circle, so the plain one can't, or the two explanations look identical.
> - **Whether the plain panel should carry the loop at all.** I argued it shouldn't, since the ring covers it; the counter-argument is that the technical panel is the one nobody understands by design, so if the loop only lives there, the episode's own word never lands in a form anyone retains.
> - **The AI's on-screen mark** — six petals round a single eye. Queried as reading like a generic AI logo. Currently appears only centred on a monitor, never over the diagram.
> - **Caption pace.** The explainer panels carry no captions now — the diagram is made of words. Everywhere else the median caption word is on screen for 299ms and 30% are under 200ms, which is below the time it takes to read one. The original plan was full sentences in a fixed bottom band; the build drifted to one word at a time and nobody decided that.
>
> **What I want opinions on:** how to represent the plain-English explanation of an agent loop, given it must look nothing like a ring-with-a-dot, must have a human in it so their removal reads on "nobody in the middle", must survive being watched muted, and must be simple enough to draw with shapes.

>
> **The problem I've spent the week on.** The AI's technical explanation plays over a screen, and that screen has to show what an agent loop is. Six attempts: three unlabelled icons *("I didn't get the story at all")*; a labelled stack, too small; a flowchart with a return edge, *abrupt* — measured at 16 visual changes in 1.9 seconds; chips in lanes, where the third chip got 0.6s against the first's 1.8s; three logos racing left to right, which reads as a download manager. **What landed: draw the loop as a loop.** One ring, "RUN A STEP" a gate at the top, "CHECK" a gate at ten o'clock, the count inside the ring because it's the loop's state, the exit shut until it fills. One dot goes round. Three revolutions, three verdicts, one failure — and on the failure the count doesn't move, so you watch a lap get spent for nothing.
>
> **Two rules that came out of it.** Let the recording set the beats — the lap length is solved from where "fail" and "passed" actually fall in the voice take, not chosen. And never advance a counter without a revolution behind it; an increment nothing caused reads as a glitch.
>
> **Technical VO:** *"Iterative execution against a termination condition. I decompose the task. Run a step. Evaluate the result. If I fail, go round and run it again. Stop when every step has passed."* Deliberately jargon-heavy — it's the explanation he *doesn't* understand, and it has to be impenetrable for "say that again like I'm not an engineer" to land two panels later.
>
> **Open:**
> - **The plain explanation's visual.** Currently a dot travelling to a chequered flag, about 180px of a 1280px frame. Being replaced. Candidates considered: robot vacuum, kettle, rabbit checking a basket, bricks laid to a string line, keys tried against a lock. Hard constraint — the technical panel already uses a thing travelling a circle, so the plain one can't, or the two explanations look identical.
> - **Whether the plain panel must carry the loop.** I argued no, the ring covers it. Counter-argument: the technical panel is the one nobody understands *by design*, so if the loop only lives there, the episode's own word never lands.
> - **The plain line**, agreed but not recorded: *"You tell me what done looks like. Then I go, check, go, check. Get it wrong, do it again. Nobody in the middle."*
> - **The AI's on-screen mark** — six petals round one eye. Queried as reading like a generic AI logo. Appears only on a monitor now, never over the diagram.
> - **Caption pace.** Explainer panels carry none — the diagram is made of words. Elsewhere the median caption word is on screen 299ms, 30% under 200ms, which is below the time it takes to read one. The original plan was full sentences in a fixed band; the build drifted to one word at a time and nobody decided that.
>
> **What I want opinions on:** how to represent the plain-English explanation of an agent loop, given it must look nothing like a ring-with-a-dot, must have a human in it so their removal reads on "nobody in the middle", must survive being watched muted, and must be simple enough to draw with shapes.
